# Generative AI in Financial Reporting

Elizabeth Blankespoor

University of Washington

Ed de Haan

Stanford University

Qianqian Li

Stanford University

February 14, 2026

## Abstract

Forthcoming, Journal of Accounting Research

Generative Artificial Intelligence (GAI) will likely alter many aspects of the financial reporting process and spawn a deep stream of academic research. We take an early step by examining the extent to which firms have begun using GAI in one important part of the reporting process: writing disclosures. We begin by evaluating a commercial tool's ability to detect GAI writing in disclosures, and we find that it reliably identifies even very small amounts of GAI usage in realistic samples. We then examine firms' actual earnings press releases, conference call prepared remarks, risk factors, MD&As, and IPO filings through 2024 and find statistically significant GAI usage in all five disclosure types, with up to 4.5% of new text written by GAI in 2024. Usage is predictably higher in the cross-section, and filings with higher GAI have systematically different linguistic properties. Our study provides early insights into the use and effects of GAI in financial reporting, and it motivates future research in this evolving area.

Keywords: Generative AI; financial reporting; large language models; GPT; textual analysis

JEL Codes: M41, O32, O33, G10

“The work of drafting an S1… might have taken a six-person team two weeks to complete, but it can now be 95 per cent done by AI in minutes.” David Solomon, CEO of Goldman Sachs (2025)

## 1. Introduction

Generative Artificial Intelligence (GAI) has improved dramatically in the last few years due to advancements in large language models. Researchers estimate half of jobs will have at least half their tasks affected by GAI, and accounting is one of the most exposed occupations (Eloundou et al. 2024). GAI will likely affect many financial reporting tasks, but given its core ability of natural language creation, examining GAI in report writing is a natural early step for accounting research. Our goal is to provide a baseline understanding of firms' use of GAI in report writing through 2024 and how that usage relates to reports' linguistic properties.

Firms face difficult tradeoffs when considering the use of GAI in financial report writing, as the technology offers both substantial benefits and significant risks. On the benefit side, firms can use GAI to reduce the effort required to draft and edit reports and potentially to improve readability, accuracy, and consistency. Higher-quality reports would facilitate users' information processing, mitigate regulatory risk, and advance the long-standing goal of plain-English reporting (SEC 1998). At the same time, agency conflicts create incentives to use GAI strategically, such as biasing tone or obfuscating negative news, and self-interested employees may use GAI to save time without supervisors' knowledge or despite explicit bans. On the cost side, firms face potentially significant costs in using GAI for report writing. Without careful oversight, using GAI risks producing inaccurate or poorly written disclosures that invite investor criticism, litigation, and regulatory penalties. The use of public GAI tools also raises concerns about inadvertent information leakage, and effective implementation requires costly process design and training and may encounter resistance from employees worried about job security.

On balance, we expect that many firms and employees use GAI in financial report

writing, especially after the launch of ChatGPT in late 2022. Supporting those expectations, a survey of 1,800 executives finds that 15% of U.S. companies had formally begun using GAI in financial reporting processes by early 2024, and another 30% were in pilot testing (KPMG 2024). Academic research finds that 23% of accountants used ChatGPT at work by February 2024 (Humlum & Vestergaard 2024), and survey evidence from 2023 indicates that 62% of respondents use ChatGPT at work, largely without their supervisors' knowledge. $ ^{1} $

We examine four types of disclosure that cover various settings, writing styles, and cost-benefit considerations to provide a broad perspective of GAI use: i) risk factors from the 10Q/K; ii) MD&A from the 10Q/K; iii) conference call prepared remarks; and iv) earnings press releases. Additional analyses examine IPO filings. Our sample includes U.S. firms from 2010 through 2024.

Our analyses use a commercial GAI-detection tool, GPTZero (Adam et al. 2024), that estimates the probability of GAI authorship in a document or sentence (variable GenScore). While the media has been skeptical of GAI detection, some research finds that GPTZero performs well in contexts such as news and social media. $ ^{2} $ However, a GAI detector’s ability can differ across contexts (Dugan et al. 2024), and prior research does not validate GPTZero within financial reports. Thus, we begin by investigating GPTZero’s detection ability within our context, and conclude that it does an impressive job of detecting GAI text in financial reports.

For example, regressions based on GenScore reliably identify when we use GAI to modify as few as 2.5% of sentences for 2.5% of firms; i.e., when just 0.0625% of all text is GAI-modified.

We next investigate the extent to which firms use GAI in actual disclosures. GPT-2 was launched in 2019 and commercial tools based on GPT-3 have been available since mid-2020 (e.g., Copy.ai and Jasper), but most firms likely did not begin using GAI until ChatGPT’s launch in November 2022. Thus, we designate disclosures from December 2022 onwards as “post-GAI” and assume, perhaps conservatively, that non-zero GenScore in the pre-GAI period is measurement error. Our regression tests use pre-GAI period data to model and isolate measurement error from true GAI usage in the post period.

The tenor of our regression results is depicted in Figure 1, which plots GenScore in new disclosure content, orthogonalized to firm-fiscal-quarter fixed effects and covariates. GenScore increases markedly in the post-GAI period, consistent with firms using GAI to write disclosures. Regressions indicate that the greatest use is in risk factors, MD&A, and the business descriptions of IPO filings, where 4.3%, 2.5% and 4.5% of new text is GAI-written in 2024. Because financial reports can be heavily reviewed and edited by humans, these estimates are likely lower bounds on the extent to which firms actually used GAI in preparing their reports.

Our second set of tests investigates cross-sectional variation in GAI usage. Consistent with predictions developed in Section 2, we find that usage is greater among: i) small firms with fewer resources; ii) firms with fewer financial reporting resources (i.e., no investor relations); iii) firms with recent significant events that require updated disclosures; and iv) firms with low audit fees. The largest differences concentrate in small versus large firms, where we find that small firms have 292% more GAI usage in conference calls and 184% more usage in press releases. Supplementary descriptive tests produce several findings, including: i) GAI usage largely occurs

in small amounts within many firms’ reports; ii) adoption is highest among technology and communications firms, and lowest among utility and energy firms; and iii) firms use GAI the most when writing about negative performance growth and special items.

Our final analyses examine the relations between GAI usage and linguistic properties of disclosures – readability, tone, and specificity – which prior research has found to affect investor information processing and market outcomes. We examine GenScore at the sentence-level and use within-report analyses to contrast the linguistic properties of content that is the least versus most likely to use GAI. GAI content tends to be more positively toned and more readable, suggesting that firms use GAI to improve clarity and spin news. Conference calls tend to be longer with no tone change, though, suggesting that relations differ for spoken content. All GAI-modified disclosures are more specific. The greater specificity and readability are likely inconsistent with GAI’s natural tendencies and thus indicate careful human prompting or editing. All of this said, we caution that identifying the causal effects of GAI is challenging due to firms’ endogenous choices about using GAI.

In sum, we find that up to 4.5% of new disclosure content is GAI-written in 2024, that usage varies predictably in the cross-section, and that GAI content tends to be more readable, tonal, and specific. Our estimates of GAI usage are nowhere near the 95% implied by the quote on page 1, and they are far below the finding in Liang et al. (2025) that up to 24% of text is GAI-written in a broad sample of corporate press releases. That earnings press releases have less GAI writing indicates that firms are more cautious in using GAI in financial reporting than in other communication contexts.

This said, several factors should be kept in mind when considering the magnitudes of our estimates. The first is that many firms likely use GAI for drafts that are then heavily edited by

humans, and we only capture GAI that remains in final reports. So, our estimates are a lower bound on total GAI use. $ ^{3} $ Second, in many settings it is likely reasonable to assume that detected usage is strongly correlated with total usage, in which case detected GenScore can proxy for broader GAI usage. Third, even small amounts of GAI use can have significant implications; e.g., damage to a firm’s reputation and returns if unauthorized GAI usage increases data risk. $ ^{4} $ Fourth, our sample ends with 2024 reports, so we provide only an early look into GAI adoption. Fifth, our results hinge on GPTZero’s measure of GenScore. Our validation tests indicate that GenScore performs remarkably well, but we cannot be certain about GenScore’s accuracy, and it is possible that future changes to GPTZero’s model could affect inferences.

The GAI surge has spurred conversations across academia, practice, and government about its potential benefits and risks, and many fields are grappling with how to responsibly harness GAI’s efficiencies while protecting stakeholders. $ ^{5} $ Our major contribution is to provide early evidence on GAI use in a critical user-centric step of the financial reporting process, writing reports, and our findings and GenScore data provide a base for further research. For example, research can investigate whether GAI in report writing generates cost savings, affects labor mix, alters other dimensions of reporting quality such as restatements, attracts regulatory

scrutiny, or affects outcomes such as readers’ information processing and market pricing. The directional effects of GAI on these outcomes are unclear, especially if firms’ or employees’ incentives are not aligned with shareholders or if GAI is implemented without sufficient oversight. Irons (2024) takes a nice step down this path by examining the association between GAI in conference calls and returns. $ ^{6} $

Future research can also examine GAI usage in other parts of the financial reporting process, e.g., gathering and analyzing source documents, recording transactions, internal controls, and substantive analysis, with potential benefits like faster period-end closes, lower costs, and fewer restatements. Our evidence that firms use GAI in report writing indicates they are likely exploring uses in other functions, and current research has begun to investigate these other uses (e.g., Choi & Xie 2025; Boke et al. 2025).

Our final contribution is methodological. As mentioned, the efficacy of GAI detection tools can vary across writing contexts and research designs. Our methods for validating GPTZero’s are (to our knowledge) novel and can be a useful precedent for future studies.

## 2. GAI in Financial Report Writing

By automating tasks and complementing human inputs, GAI has the potential to lower the cost of writing financial reports while simultaneously improving their quality (Acemoglu 2024; Acemoglu and Restrepo 2019). On the cost side, automation manifests as employees using GAI to perform routine tasks, such as drafting disclosures, organizing ideas, revising language across multiple iterations, and adapting report content to different formats. Regarding quality,

practitioners describe using GAI as a writing partner that improves content beyond what they could do alone, such as by identifying points of confusion, resolving disagreements among report writers and executives, and anticipating questions from diverse stakeholders. GAI can also improve reports directly through clear, accurate exposition and indirectly by providing better inputs and freeing employee time for higher value report-related tasks. Consistent with this view, experimental evidence in a non-financial reporting context shows that GAI assistance improves writing quality by 18 percent while reducing writing time by 40 percent (Noy and Zhang 2023). Prior research also documents the difficulty of communicating complex topics in plain English (e.g., Dyer et al. 2017), underscoring GAI's potential to meaningfully improve financial communications.

There are also costs of using GAI in financial reporting. First, reports of GAI inaccuracies abound. $ ^{7} $ For firms discussing material information for investors, errors like a wrong date or amount could result in an apology and retraction in the best case, and a lawsuit or penalties in a worse scenario. Second, GAI wording may be biased, inappropriate, inconsistent with the firm’s style, or irrelevant and off-topic (e.g., Choi et al. 2023). Erroneous content is especially problematic in capital markets where words are scrutinized for market-moving information. Third, there are privacy concerns with using GAI on a third-party server, especially for information that competitors or traders could profit from. Developing in-house tools for proprietary data is costly and thus a barrier to GAI adoption. Finally, employees may be reticent to use GAI, due to both learning costs and concerns of job loss.

Given the substantial potential benefits, we expect many firms and/or employees use GAI

to write financial reports despite the costs, with usage concentrating after the launch of ChatGPT on 30 November 2022. $ ^{8} $ Using GAI in report writing leverages its core strength of natural language generation, and risks from hallucinations or mathematical inaccuracies can be mitigated via human oversight. Moreover, our conversations with practitioners highlighted that using GAI in writing is relatively easy to do using chat boxes, as compared to system integrations that are often required to use GAI in other reporting processes.

The implications of GAI usage for disclosure quality, investor processing, and firm value depend on whether firm, employee, and investor interests are aligned. In the absence of agency frictions, GAI usage likely benefits investors by improving report quality and/or reducing costs. If firms or employees are instead motivated to obfuscate negative news or to shirk, then GAI usage could harm investors.

### 2.1 Cross-sectional variation in GAI adoption

Firms will adopt GAI when the expected benefits exceed the costs. We investigate four related determinants of that cost-benefit decision.

We first investigate small firms that tend to have fewer resources. Because a primary benefit of GAI is to complete tasks using fewer resources, we expect small firms are more likely to adopt GAI to write reports. For example, the employees of small firms likely have more diverse responsibilities and fewer options for support, so are more likely to use a low-cost tool that can mimic an extra writer. A source of tension is that small firms are likely less able to afford in-house GAI that mitigates privacy concerns, but our conversations with practitioners

indicate that in-house tools were generally uncommon during most of our sample period.

Second, we investigate firms that have fewer resources specifically for financial reporting. Firms without an investor relations (IR) officer likely dedicate fewer resources to financial reporting, so would especially benefit from low-cost GAI assistance in report writing. However, firms that choose not to have an IR officer may care little about financial reporting and find that the costs of using GAI exceed its benefits. It is also possible that IR officers are uniquely positioned to appreciate the value of GAI, in which case firms with IR officers would be more likely to adopt GAI.

Third, we expect that firms are more likely to use GAI when recent economic events require significant disclosure updates. For example, after a major divestment or acquisition, a firm likely needs to revise many of its disclosures and is thus more likely to use GAI.

Fourth, we investigate firms with low size-adjusted audit fees. Low investment in audit services could mean the firm dedicates fewer resources to financial reporting and has less access to external experts. In that case, firms with low audit fees likely use GAI to overcome these barriers. However, low audit fees could instead be driven by firms having fewer complex events or stronger reporting systems, in which cases there may be less GAI use. Thus, the directional prediction is not entirely clear.

### 2.2 Linguistic properties of GAI-modified disclosure

Research finds that linguistic properties affect information processing, stakeholder transactions, and stock market outcomes (Blankespoor et al. 2020), so it is important to understand how GAI affects reports' linguistic properties. In practice, the effects of GAI likely depend on: users' GAI prompts; the inherent tendencies of large language models (LLMs); and the extent to which GAI text is edited by humans. Here we motivate predictions based on firms'

incentives and GAI capabilities, but the relations could easily differ across firms.

We first consider disclosure readability. Longer, less readable disclosures have higher processing costs (e.g., You & Zhang 2009; Miller 2010), and the SEC recommends plain-English language (e.g., SEC 1998). GAI use could reduce length and improve readability. Bernard et al. (2026) find that disclosure readability improves when ChatGPT is carefully prompted. Further, many LLMs are skilled at summarization (e.g., Zhang et al. 2024a) and avoid generating very long text blocks (e.g., Que et al. 2024), although perhaps at the cost of informativeness due to models ignoring the middle of prompts (Wan et al. 2024). However, firms could also use GAI to obfuscate bad news with complex language (e.g., Bushee et al. 2018; deHaan et al. 2021), and research finds that LLMs prompted to revise short text passages can sometimes lengthen them or produce unnecessarily verbose text (e.g., Shu et al. 2024; Zhang et al. 2024b).

Second, we consider tone. The sign and intensity of disclosure tone convey information (e.g., Li 2010; Bochkay et al. 2020), so firms have incentives to use GAI to positively spin reports to influence the market (e.g., Huang et al. 2014; Blankespoor & deHaan 2020). However, research finds mixed evidence of GAI amplifying tonal language and of dampening tone towards neutral, suggesting that the effects differ depending on context, model, and prompt (e.g., Lei et al. 2024; Wang et al. 2025; Gandhi and Gandhi 2025).

Finally, we consider specificity. More specific reports, such as those with more numbers and proper nouns, tend to have greater information content (e.g., Hope et al. 2016) and lower information processing costs (e.g., Huang et al. 2018; Liberti & Petersen 2019). GAI can reduce the cost of preparing detailed disclosures, but those details must be provided by managers. Without careful prompting or editing, GAI could easily produce less specific content.

## 3. Sample and Data

We examine GAI use in four disclosures that have different styles and create different incentives to use GAI. First, we examine risk factors from 10Q/K reports (“RF”) because they have a highly structured format and often legalistic style with room to improve their readability. However, the text is often the same year-over-year (i.e., ~79% stickiness year-over-year), reducing opportunities for GAI use. Our second disclosure is MD&As from 10Q/K reports. This section is still structured but tends to be less legalistic and more likely to require adjustments each year (~69% stickiness). Our third disclosure is transcripts from the presentation portion of quarterly conference calls (“CC”). These are written in a casual style designed to be spoken, and the language changes over time (~20% stickiness). Our fourth disclosure is quarterly earnings press releases (“PR”), which have a more informal and persuasive style (~62% stickiness).

As detailed in Table 1A, our sample includes firms with fiscal period-ends from 2010 to 2024. Starting in 2010 provides a long pre-GAI period for modeling temporal trends. 10Q/K data and press releases are from WRDS SEC Analytics Suite, and conference call transcripts are from Capital IQ. There are fewer observations for risk factors than MD&A because firms are only required to report quarterly risk factors when there are changes from the last year-end.

The primary variable in most of our analyses is GenScore, which is the probability of GAI usage in a report. GPTZero assesses GAI authorship using a proprietary algorithm, and perplexity and burstiness are key parts of their assessment. $ ^{9} $ Low perplexity sentences include words that a GAI model is more likely to suggest in context, and GAI models write in a more uniform phrase length and style that has lower burstiness than humans. GPTZero is designed to detect GAI usage from a variety of LLMs regardless of whether they are accessed from web

front-ends or APIs. Table 1B shows that GenScore averages 0.55% across the sample.

Our GPTZero validation tests are computationally intensive and so are based on a balanced panel of year-end reports for firms from 2018 through 2020. $ ^{10} $ Specifically, for each disclosure type, we randomly select 50 firms from each decile of 2019 market value, for a total of 500 firms and 1,500 reports per disclosure type. We use the first 5,000 words from each report, rounding up to finish the last sentence. $ ^{11} $

## 4. GAI Detection Validation: GPTZero Types I & II Errors

Our primary analyses are based on variations of the following OLS model:

 $$ \begin{array}{r l}{G e n S c o r e_{i t}}&{=\beta_{l}P o s t_{t}+F i r m-F Q\;F E_{i}+\varepsilon_{i t}}\end{array} $$ 

GenScore is the probability of GAI usage for a report for firm i in period t. Post is an indicator for the post-GAI period (December 2022 and onwards). Fixed effects for each firm and fiscal quarter (i.e., FQ 1, 2, 3, 4) restrict analyses to within-firm seasonally-adjusted differences. See Section 5 for further details.

We investigate the internal validity of model (1) by assessing its risks of generating types I and II errors. Table 2 presents GenScore summary statistics for our validation subsample. The mean GenScore is up to 0.5% across disclosure types, and is presumably measurement error if firms were not using GAI through 2020.

There are two potential drivers of type I errors in model (1). The first is abnormal statistical properties of GenScore and model (1), but randomization tests in Section S2 of the

Online Appendix show no evidence of excessive errors in the validation sample. The second is a

temporal trend in GenScore, but tests in Section 5 examining the full sample conclude that trends

are unlikely to drive material type I errors in our regression framework.

### 4.1 Power analysis

We gauge type II error rates by inserting known amounts of GAI text into firms' reports and testing whether model (1) can detect them. Our goal is to create GAI-modified reports that mimic using GAI to improve human drafts. We use two approaches to insert GAI text into reports: document-level and sentence-level replacement. Specifically, we provide GPT-3.5 with a firm's full report and then each sentence, and prompt it to improve clarity while maintaining similar format and length. $ ^{12} $

#### 4.1.1 Power analysis: document-level replacement

Our document-level tests replace firms’ actual reports with a version modified by GAI. Table 3A presents estimated overlap between the reports, using the percent of phrases in the modified report that also appear in the original. Overlap ranges from 4.1% to 41%. Table 3B reports GenScore in the GAI-modified reports. The mean GenScore of 97.1% for RF indicates that 97% of the document has GAI-written text. Thus, GenScore accurately identifies GAI-modified report text, even though 24% of modified sentences in RFs contain human-written phrases (Table 3A). Results are similar for MD&A, CC, and PR.

Because our objective is to gauge power in our primary tests, our power analyses use the full sample sizes from Table 1A. Our procedures are as follows:

• For each firm in the full sample, assign it a random match from our validation sample. Use

the validation firm’s unmodified GenScores to randomly populate the firm’s time series.

- Designate all period-ends from 1 December 2022 onwards as Induced-Post = 1. Exclude any firms that do not appear in both the pre- and post-GAI periods. $ ^{13} $

- Choose an adoption parameter,  $ \alpha $, which is the fraction of firms that adopt GAI in the post period. Start with  $ \alpha = 100\% $.

- For  $ \alpha $ of the firms, replace the unmodified report GenScore values for Induced-Post = 1 periods with the GAI-modified report GenScore values. Thus,  $ \alpha $ firms’ reports are GAI-modified in the Induced-Post period, while  $ (1 - \alpha) $ firms’ reports are not GAI-modified.

• Estimate model (1) with Induced-Post. Save the  $ \beta_{1} $ estimate and standard error.

• Repeat these steps 100 times to estimate a distribution of  $ \beta_{1} $ for  $ \alpha = 100\% $.

Table 3C reports that for  $ \alpha = 100\% $ for RF, the average Induced-Post coefficient is 96.61 and 100% of trials reject the null, indicating that model (1) accurately identifies GAI-modified reports. The Induced-Post coefficients decrease proportionately as  $ \alpha $ decreases. Even with  $ \alpha = 1\% $ for RF, the null is rejected in 100% of trials. Results are similar for MD&A, CC, and PR.

4.1.2 Power analysis: sentence-level replacement

Our sentence-level replacement tests mimic a firm that uses GAI to modify small amounts of text or when humans heavily edit GAI-written content. These tests use the simulated sample from Section 4.1.1 but differ from the document-level replacement tests in two ways. First, we ask GAI to modify one sentence at a time. Second, we set a usage rate parameter  $ \mu $ to designate the fraction of sentences from the original report to be replaced with GAI-modified versions. For example, when  $ \mu = 10\% $, we mimic a firm that uses GAI in 10% of the report.

Table 3D provides rejection rates from the sentence-level replacement tests. The upper left cell shows that for  $ \alpha = 100\% $ and  $ \mu = 100\% $ in RF, 100% of trials are rejected. Moving rightward across columns shows that the rejection rate remains at 100% even for low adoption rates. Rejection rates for RF remain above 80%, a standard power threshold, even for low usage rates. For example, when adoption is only 5% ( $ \alpha = 5\% $) and those firms use GAI in 5% of sentences ( $ \mu = 5\% $), just 0.25% of text is modified in the Post period, yet model (1) still reliably rejects the null in 98% of trials for RF. The results are slightly stronger for MD&A, CC, and PR.

We further improve power by isolating new disclosure content. Specifically, we remove any sentence that contains a six-word phrase repeated from the prior year, and then rerun the trials from Table 3D. Results in Table 3E show that removing sticky text improves power; model (1) now identifies GAI usage as low as  $ \alpha = 2.5\% $ and  $ \mu = 2.5\% $ for RF, MD&A, and PR.

### 4.2 The performance of binary classifications

Our primary analyses investigate the continuous GenScore, but other use-cases require a binary classification. We therefore convert GenScore to a binary measure and characterize GPTZero’s ability to differentiate our 6,000 human-written reports from their GAI-modified counterparts. See Section S3 of the Online Appendix for additional details.

We use two methods to convert GenScore into an indicator. The first uses a GenScore threshold,  $ \gamma $, above which a document is deemed to be GAI-modified. Specifically, we first mitigate measurement error by de-meaning GenScore by firm and disclosure type in human-written reports, which mimics the functionality of the fixed effects in model (1). We then set  $ \gamma $ as the  $ 90^{th} $ percentile of demeaned GenScore among human-written documents, which equates to a 10% type I error rate. All observations, human and GAI-modified, with demeaned GenScore >  $ \gamma $ are designated as GAI-modified. Figures 2A and 2B plot precision, recall, and F1 scores when

the fraction of GAI-modified sentences varies from  $ \mu = 1\% $ to 100%, pooling the disclosure types for brevity. $ ^{14} $ F1-scores in Figure 2B indicate that this method is acceptable for  $ \mu \geq 10\% $.

Our second classification method acknowledges that researchers may not have human-written documents to determine  $ \gamma $, in which case an alternative is to use a within-sample ranking of GenScore. We pool the human and GAI-modified reports by disclosure type, rank GenScore into terciles, and identify the upper third as high GAI observations, lower third as low GAI, and omit the middle group. $ ^{15} $ “High” and “low” classification is not as stringent as whether a report does or does not contain GAI-modified text. Figure 2B shows that F1 scores are acceptable for all levels of  $ \mu $ when using a tercile-based classification.

## 5. Analyses of GAI Usage

We investigate firms’ GAI usage using model (1) for the full sample from 2010 through 2024. As before, GenScore is the probability of GAI usage. Post is an indicator for period-ends after 30 November 2022. To mitigate the possibility that firms use ChatGPT in reports for periods-ends before 1 December 2022, we exclude period-ends from 1 September through 30 November 2022. Firm-fiscal-quarter fixed effects in (1) help remove seasonal, firm-specific measurement error in GenScore that exists even in the pre period. We also control for time-varying characteristics: firm size, book-to-market, analyst following, institutional ownership, leverage, number of segments, an indicator for recent material M&A, and a loss indicator. These characteristics capture various firm fundamentals, capital market incentives, and economic

activities. Standard errors are clustered by year-quarter. $ ^{16} $ If firms use GAI to write an economically meaningful portion of reports in the post period, we expect  $ \beta_{1} > 0 $.

An identifying assumption in model (1) is that the change in GenScore would have been flat in the absence of the introduction of ChatGPT. Figure 3A plots raw GenScore over time and displays an unexpected slight upward trend starting in 2020, before ChatGPT’s launch. Pre-ChatGPT trends could have two main explanations. The first is that firms were using tools based on early LLMs, so the pre-period increase in GenScore reflects real usage. For example, GPT-3’s API was launched in June 2020, and commercial products using it became available shortly after. The second explanation is that LLMs are trained to mimic more recent data, so secular trends in writing and speech cause increases in GenScore over time. This latter explanation would indicate that some of the trend in GenScore is non-random measurement error. $ ^{17} $

Figure 3B plots GenScore after it is orthogonalized to firm-fiscal-quarter fixed effects and controls, which is the residual variation used to estimate  $ \beta_{1} $ in model (1). The time trend from Figure 3A is largely eliminated, indicating that much of the trend is due to systematic measurement error that is removed by our regression design. $ ^{18} $ Figure 1 repeats Figure 3B using only new text within firms’ reports (i.e., after removing sticky sentences from the prior year). The pattern in Figure 1 provides minimal evidence of an increase in GenScore before late 2022, followed by sharp increases in 2023 and 2024. Overall, pre-GAI time trends in GenScore do not appear to be a major identification threat given our regression design, but tests below further

investigate the robustness of our findings to time trends.

Results of model (1) are in Table 4A. $ ^{19} $ The  $ \beta_{1} $ coefficients range from 0.318 for RF to 1.167 for CC, indicating that 0.32% to 1.17% of content is GAI-written in the post period.

Because GAI adoption likely takes time, we also examine individual year indicators:

 $$ \begin{aligned}GenScore_{it}&=\beta_{1}Year2024_{t}+\beta_{2}Year2023_{t}+\beta_{3}Late2022_{t}+Controls_{it}+Firm-FQ\ FE_{i}\\&+\varepsilon_{it}\end{aligned} $$ 

Late2022 equals one for period-ends in December 2022, after ChatGPT’s launch. Year2024 and Year2023 are indicators for reports in 2024 and 2023. If GAI adoption takes time, we expect  $ \beta_{1} > \beta_{2} > \beta_{3} $. Results in Table 4B indicate that GAI usage increases monotonically across the years, reaching 0.51% for RF and 1.43% for CC in 2024. GAI usage is nominally greater for CC and PR, likely because they have more year-over-year changes and possibly because of less stringent reviews from accountants and lawyers.

We next add a linear time trend, TimeTrend, to further control for trends in GenScore:

 $$ \begin{aligned}GenScore_{it}&=\beta_{1}Year2024_{t}+\beta_{2}Year2023_{t}+\beta_{3}Late2022_{t}+\beta_{4}TimeTrend_{t}+Controls_{it}\\&+Firm-FQFE_{i}+\varepsilon_{it}\end{aligned} $$ 

Results in Table 4C find a significantly positive but economically small coefficient on TimeTrend for all disclosures. More importantly, the annual coefficients remain significantly positive, consistent with a discontinuous increase in GAI usage in the post period. Comparing Panels B and C shows that the Year2024 coefficient magnitudes decrease by roughly 10%, which is relatively modest. A more notable difference is that many of the statistically significant Controls in Panels A and B become insignificant in Panel C, indicating that they do not have

robust relations with GenScore once TimeTrend is controlled. $ ^{20} $

Table 4D estimates model (3) but removes sticky language from the prior year. This approach identifies GAI-written text in new disclosure content, but we lose observations due to missing lagged reports or because remaining non-sticky text is too short for GPTZero. Controls are untabulated for brevity. The Year2024 coefficient for RF increases from 0.454 in Panel C to 4.258 in Panel D, indicating that GAI usage is highly concentrated in new RF disclosures. That the increase in  $ \beta_{1} $ is largest for RF is logical given these are the stickiest disclosure year-over-year. Still, effect sizes for MD&A and PR all more than double from Panel C to D.

### 5.1 Cross-sectional predictions

Our cross-sectional tests investigate which firms are most likely to use GAI. A secondary benefit is that these tests use a difference-in-differences (DiD) design that further mitigates concerns about time trends. Our DiD extension of model (3) is as follows:

 $$ \begin{array}{r l}{GenScore_{it}}&{=\beta_{1}Year2024_{t}*XSec_{it}+\beta_{2}Year2023_{t}*XSec_{it}+\beta_{3}Late2022_{t}*XSec_{it}}\\ &{+\beta_{4}Year2024_{t}+\beta_{5}Year2023_{t}+\beta_{6}Late2022_{t}+\beta_{7}XSec_{it}+\beta_{8}TimeTrend_{t}}\\ &{+\beta_{9}TimeTrend_{t}*XSec_{it}+Controls_{it}+Firm-FQ\;F E_{i}+\varepsilon_{i t}}\end{array} $$ 

XSec is an indicator to identify a cross-sectional group. XSec varies within-firm so is not absorbed by the firm-fiscal-quarter fixed effects. TimeTrend*XSec controls for any linear time trend that differs for the XSec = 1 firms. $ ^{21} $ We tabulate results excluding sticky text to focus on likely new report content, similar to analyses in 4D, and results using the complete reports are in Section S4 in the Online Appendix.

As discussed in Section 2.1, we expect that GAI usage is greater among smaller firms,

firms without an IR officer, firms with more significant recent events, and firms with lower audit fees. We identify Small = 1 as firms in the smallest tercile of market value calculated by year; all other firms are Small = 0. We identify IR officers that appear in earnings conference call transcripts (Chapman et al. 2019, 2022), and code No IR = 1 for firms that do not have an internal officer. We identify economic events based on firms with large absolute abnormal returns during the period. Abnormal returns should capture a range of economic events (e.g., significant growth, devaluation, M&A, product launches, etc.), without requiring proxies for each event. Firms in the largest tercile of absolute abnormal returns are designated as Large Returns = 1. We identify Low Audit Fee = 1 if firms are in the smallest tercile of size-adjusted fees during the fiscal year. If XSec = 1 firms make greater use of GAI, we expect the interaction coefficients  $ \beta_{1} $ through  $ \beta_{3} $ from model (4) to be positive.

Table 5A presents results for small firms. Year2024, Year2023, and Late2022 are generally positive and significant, indicating that GAI usage increases on average in all four disclosures. As expected, smaller firms generally increase GAI usage more than larger firms. Eight of 12 Small interaction coefficients are positive, with seven significant at the 10% level or better. The largest incremental effects are for PR and CC. In CC, small firms use GAI in an estimated ( $ \beta_1 + \beta_4 = 3.7\% $ of their reports in 2024 while large firms use GAI in ( $ \beta_4 = 0.9\% $, representing roughly 300% higher usage. In PR, small firms use GAI in an estimated 4.4% of new text in 2024, versus 1.6% for large firms. Small firms also use GAI significantly more than large firms in MD&A, but not for RF. Importantly,  $ \beta_9 $ reveals no meaningful differences in TimeTrend for small firms, meaning that the DiD structure holds constant any linear trends in GenScore. All four TimeTrend*Small coefficients are small, three are statistically insignificant, and the fourth is significantly negative. Thus, if anything, Small firms have a negative trend in

GenScore which biases against finding a positive DiD.

Results in Table 5B for No IR are similar as for Small firms but a bit smaller in economic magnitude. GAI usage is significantly greater among No IR firms across all four disclosures and in 10 of 12 interaction coefficients. The incremental increase in GAI usage is again highest for PR, where No IR firms use GAI in 3.0% of new content in 2024 while firms with IR have just 1.7%. We do not observe economically meaningful differences in TimeTrend across firms with and without internal IR (see  $ \beta_{9} $). Overall, the results in Table 5B are consistent with GAI usage being greater among firms that do not have internal IR officers.

Table 5C presents results for Large Returns. GAI usage is significantly greater among Large Returns firms for CC and PR, but not for MD&A or RF. Table 5D finds more GAI usage for firms with lower fee audits. Unlike prior panels, the effect size is now largest for RF, where firms with low fees use GAI in 5.8% of new RF content in 2024 versus 3.7% for firms with high audit fees.

In sum, the results in Table 5 are generally consistent with our predictions about how firms weigh the costs and benefits of using GAI in financial reporting. Most tests find that firms with stronger net incentives use GAI more heavily in CCs and PRs, and MD&As to a slightly lesser degree. Firms with low audit fees also have incrementally greater usage in RFs. $ ^{22} $ Untabulated results in Section S4 in the Online Appendix using the complete reports are similar, with slightly stronger (weaker) results for the small firm and large absolute return (IR and audit fees) tests.

### 5.2 GAI usage in IPO S1 filings

Table 6 investigates firms’ initial public offering (IPO) S1 filings. On the one hand, GAI

usage is plausibly high in S1 filings, given they require extensive new text. On the other hand, S1 filings are likely especially heavily edited, so GAI usage in early drafts might be undetectable in final drafts. We focus on four sections: Business Description (BUS), MD&A, Risk Factors (RF), and Prospectus Summary (PS). $ ^{23} $ We investigate S1s using a version of model (3) but without firm fixed effects, given that each firm has just one filing. We also exclude Controls because roughly half the IPOs are unsuccessful or over-the-counter, so do not have Compustat or CRSP data. Table 6 documents significant increases in GenScore for BUS, MD&A, and PS. The largest effect is for BUS, for which an estimated 4.48% of text is GAI-written in 2024. $ ^{24} $

### 5.3 Additional descriptive analyses of GAI usage

This section further describes variation in GAI usage, without ex ante predictions.

Table 7A examines the distribution of GenScore in the post-GAI period to get a sense of the extensive versus intensive margins of GAI usage. To eliminate pre-GAI measurement error, we calculate  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $ as GenScore in each post-GAI report minus the firm's average GenScore in the pre period for that disclosure type and fiscal quarter. The  $ 99^{th} $ percentiles of  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $ are relatively low, ranging from 4.9 to 17.5, indicating that many firms use GAI in moderate amounts (the extensive margin).

Table 7B investigates the extensive margin of GAI adoption by year. To identify GAI “adopters” we: i) average  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $ by firm and disclosure type within each year; ii) split the averages into terciles; and iii) label a firm as an “adopter” if it falls into the highest tercile of

∆GenScore. Non-adopters are those always in the bottom tercile of ∆GenScore, and all other firms are excluded. $ ^{25} $ Table 7B shows that 23% of these firms first adopt GAI in RF in late 2022, 32% in 2023, 17% in 2024, and 29% do not adopt by sample-end. The pattern is similar for MD&A, although fewer firms remain in the “non-adopter” group. Adoption for CC and PR is faster, which is plausibly because CC and PR require more updates each period and are less formal than RF and MD&A.

Table 7C describes the firms designated as adopters and non-adopters. We use data from 2019 through 2021 to measure ex ante characteristics, and we pool the four disclosure types for brevity. Similar to our earlier cross-sectional tests (which examine overall GAI usage), firms at the extensive margin of GAI adoption are smaller, less likely to have IR, more likely to have large returns, and more likely to have low audit fees. Adopters also have fewer analysts, less institutional ownership, and fewer segments, and are more likely to be loss firms, consistent with resource constraints motivating GAI adoption.

Table 7D repeats 7C but presents firm characteristics by adoption year. The average differences between adopters and non-adopters from Table 7C also exist for each year of adopters; for example, adopters in late 2022, 2023, and 2024 are all smaller than non-adopters. However, the data in Table 7D do not provide clear evidence of monotonic trends across the adoption years. Rather, the averages fluctuate between adoption years.

We next examine differences in GAI usage across industries. We first average winsorized  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $ by GICS2 industry, and then rank the industries from 1 (high) through 11 (low).

Table 7E shows that information technology firms have the highest GAI usage, consistent with tech firms being more willing to embrace new technology, followed by communications firms. Utilities use GAI the least, which is plausibly due to their regulatory oversight and atypical capital market pressures. The next lowest industries are energy and financial firms.

Our final analyses use latent dirichlet allocation to characterize how GAI usage varies across topics within reports. We classify each sentence into one of 55 topics that we map into labels from Dyer et al. (2017) where possible. $ ^{26} $ See Section S5 in the Online Appendix for more details. We then calculate the average GenScore for each topic j, and calculate a within-topic  $ \Delta GenScore_{jt} $ as the average GenScore in the post period minus the pre period. We limit these analyses to the MD&A because it discusses the widest range of topics and is available for nearly all firm-quarters. Figure 4A plots average within-topic  $ \Delta GenScore_{jt} $ and shows that GAI usage is greatest when discussing “performance growth (negative)” or “special items.” This is consistent with firms using GAI to write about new or unusual items, especially negative news. $ ^{27} $ GAI usage is low in technical accounting topics with more boilerplate language, such as “fair value/impairment” and “non-gaap & adjusted performance measures.” Many of the other topics relate to industry-specific considerations.

Figure 4B focuses on the industry with the most GAI usage: Information Technology. We classify sentences into 30 topics within the Information Technology industry, and we find that the top GAI-written topics are similar to the full sample: “performance growth” and “special items,” along with “equity financing” (#3 in the full sample) and “capital investment and share

repurchase” (a topic related to equity financing and special items). The least GAI-written topics again include technical accounting topics.

## 6. GAI and Financial Reporting Linguistic Properties

We examine the relations between GAI usage and readability, tone, and specificity using proxies from prior literature. For readability, we use the Fog score and logged number of words (Li 2008; Miller 2010). For tone, we use FinBERT sentiment from Huang et al. (2023). For specificity, we use the extent of named entities and quantitative disclosure (Hope et al. 2016).

6.1 Linguistic differences in the validation sample

We begin by investigating the linguistic properties of our validation sample. While the effects of GAI on linguistics will depend on the prompts used and extent of human editing, our validation sample can provide insight into the effects of GAI in one use-case. Table 8A reports average properties of the human-written reports. We contrast the properties of the human-written and GAI-modified reports using the following regression:

 $$ \begin{array}{r l}{L i n g u i s t i c_{i t v}}&{=\beta_{l}G A I-M o d i f i e d_{i t v}+R e p o r t\;F E_{i t}+\varepsilon_{i t v}}\end{array} $$ 

Linguistic is the linguistic property of version v of a report for firm i in period t, where v refers to the human-written versus GAI-modified version of the report. Report fixed effects restrict analysis to the two versions of the same report, and in doing so absorb firm fixed effects, controls, and time trends. GAI-Modified is an indicator for the GAI version of the report, and  $ \beta_{1} $ estimates the within-report difference in linguistic property.

Table 8B reports the  $ \beta_{1} $ estimates for each linguistic property and for both the document-level and sentence-level replacement methods.  $ \beta_{1} $ for the length of document-level GAI modifications ranges from -0.174 to -0.628, and all are statistically significant. Compared to the human-written reports in Table 8A, these coefficients indicate that document-level GAI modified

reports are 17% – 63% shorter. Signs reverse for the length of sentence-level modified reports, with modified reports being 19% – 36% longer. Together, these results suggest that when a GAI is prompted to improve the clarity of financial report writing while retaining the main points, its tendency is to moderate length, lengthening shorter passages and shortening longer passages.

The results for other properties are consistent across both methods: GAI modifications tend to be foggier (0.3% – 26%), more tonal (5% – 203%), and less specific (1% – 27%). $ ^{28} $ For tone, GAI amplifies the existing tone, with the typically negative RF (see Table 8A) becoming more negative, and the typically positive MD&A, CC, and PR becoming more positive. Lower specificity is unsurprising given we do not provide additional specifics in the prompt. $ ^{29} $

### 6.2 Linguistic differences in GAI content in firms' actual reports

In firms’ actual filings, an identification challenge is that reports’ linguistic properties and GAI usage could both be affected by firms’ economic events. For example, an acquisition likely requires a longer 10K and increases the probability that the firm uses GAI to help write the acquisition disclosures. In this example, GAI may reduce disclosure length relative to an acquisition disclosure not written with GAI, but the change in length from the prior year is likely still positive. We use several techniques to help hold constant the endogenous drivers of GAI usage, but we still caution against overly strong inferences.

Our empirical approach aims to resemble our analyses of the validation sample, for which we could tightly contrast the linguistic properties of two versions of the same report. Because we do not have a human-written and GAI-modified version of each actual report, we instead contrast

two sections within each report that are more versus less likely to use GAI. Restricting to within-report variation allows us to hold constant firm events and other factors that affect both filing-level linguistic properties and GAI use. $ ^{30} $

We use GPTZero’s sentence-level GenScore to identify the sentences with the top and bottom 10% of GAI within each report. Given our findings that firms only use GAI in portions of reports, the bottom 10% of GenScore sentences are almost certainly human-written. We assume that the top 10% of GenScore sentences have GAI usage in the post period, but we revisit this assumption below. We then calculate the linguistic properties of each group of sentences, creating two observations per report. We contrast the two groups using the following regression:

 $$ \begin{array}{r l}{L i n g u i s t i c_{i t g}}&{=\beta_{l}P o s t_{t}*T o p_{i t g}+\beta_{2}T i m e T r e n d_{t}*T o p_{i t g}+R e p o r t\;F E_{i t}}\\ &{+F i r m-F Q\;F E_{i t}*T o p_{i t g}+\varepsilon_{i t g}}\end{array} $$ 

In Model (6), subscript g designates a group of sentences from each report, and Top is an indicator for the group with the top 10% of GenScores. Report fixed effects restrict comparison to the top and bottom group of sentences within the same report. In doing so, they eliminate any firm-period-level factor that could affect the linguistics of all sentences within a report (e.g., a firm's size or performance), any common time trends in linguistics, the main effects of Post and TimeTrend, and the Firm-FQ FE. Firm-fiscal-quarter fixed effects interacted with Top eliminate static differences between each firm's Top = 1 and Top = 0 sentences, and absorb the main effect of Top. We allow for the possibility that Top = 0 and Top = 1 sentences have different time trends by interacting TimeTrend with Top. This design allows us to use  $ \beta_{1} $ to compare GAI language to human language within a report, using the pre-ChatGPT period to reduce

measurement concerns.

Table 9A presents the results of model (6) for length. The sample size is slightly smaller than two observations per report due to minimum length requirements. The  $ \beta_{1} $ coefficients indicate that GAI content within RF, MD&A, and PR is roughly 2.5% to 19.2% shorter than human-written content, suggesting it is more readable. CCs are slightly longer, although  $ \beta_{1} $ is statistically and economically small. The  $ \beta_{2} $ coefficients provide some evidence that Top sentences have a different time trend in length, although the magnitudes are modest.

Table 9B presents results for fog and provides similar inferences: RF, MD&A, and PR sections that contain GAI usage tend to be more readable than human-written sections. CCs are again the exception with an insignificant  $ \beta_{1} $ coefficient. Table 9C finds that sections of RF, MD&A, and PR that have more GAI usage tend to have more positive tone, and again results are insignificant for CC. Table 9D finds that GAI sections are more specific in all disclosure types.

An empirical weakness of model (6) is that it assumes that Top = 1 sentences are GAI-written in the post period, but some firms may have zero GAI usage. In such cases, we introduce measurement error by comingling observations for GAI users and non-users. Analyses in Section S7 of the Online Appendix mitigate this measurement error by restricting analyses to firms that likely use GAI. In brief, we identify likely GAI users as firms in the top tercile of within-firm change in GenScore from the pre to post periods. Rerunning Tables 9A through 9D using only likely GAI users finds qualitatively unchanged results despite smaller samples.

Overall, the results in Table 9 indicate that sections of RF, MD&A, and PR with more GAI usage tend to be more readable, more positive, and more specific. The tone results are generally consistent with firms' incentives to use GAI to spin news. Improved readability is consistent with managers using GAI to improve clarity. These results contrast with Table 8's

evidence that simple GAI prompts decrease readability, which suggests managers are careful in prompting or editing. Similarly, simple GAI prompts do not inherently increase specificity (as shown in Table 8B), so the results in Table 9D indicate that managers intervene with prompting or editing to provide specific details. It is also possible that GAI efficiencies enable greater inclusion of details; e.g., through GAI-aided analysis or by providing managers more time for editing. CC are the exception, where GAI language has no difference in tone or readability. This indicates that GAI usage differs for content spoken by managers, perhaps because managers' preferences and scrutiny play a larger role. All of this said, we again caution against drawing strong inferences about linguistic characteristics given identification challenges.

## 7. Conclusion

We investigate the extent and effects of GAI usage in writing financial reports. We first validate the ability of GPTZero to detect GAI in firms' reports, and then examine GAI in firms' conference calls, earnings press releases, risk disclosures, MD&A, and IPO filings through 2024. We find meaningful GAI usage in all five disclosure types, with greater use by firms with fewer resources or more new events to write about. We also find evidence that GAI-written text is more readable, positive, and specific, but identification challenges prevent us from drawing strong inferences about causal effects. Our study provides early, large-sample evidence on GAI use in writing financial reports, and it helps motivate what we expect will become a deep stream of research on GAI in many aspects of firms' financial reporting processes.

Zhang, Y., Das, S.S.S. and Zhang, R., 2024b. Verbosity  $ \neq $ Veracity: Demystify Verbosity Compensation Behavior of Large Language Models. arXiv preprint arXiv:2411.07858.

#### Appendix A: Variable Definitions

Continuous variables other than GenScore are winsorized at 1% and 99% by year-quarter and disclosure type. Periodic variables (e.g., M&A during the period) are measured during the fiscal quarter for variables based on the conference call, earnings announcement, or 10Q filing, and are measured during the fiscal year for variables based on the 10K filing.

word-wrap: break-word;'>Generated</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Length</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Logged number of words in the disclosure.</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>WRDS SEC Analytics, Capital IQ</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Fog</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>FOG index = 0.4*(average number of words per sentence + percent of complex words), where complex words are words with more than two syllables.</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>WRDS SEC Analytics, Capital IQ</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Specificity</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Number of entities (locations, people, organizations, dollar amounts, percentages, dates, or times) identified by the Stanford Named Entity Recognizer (NER), scaled by total words. Ratio is multiplied by 1,000.</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>WRDS SEC Analytics, Capital IQ, Stanford NER</td></tr></table>





<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Figure 1. GenScore Over Time Within New Disclosure Content</div> </div>


This chart depicts GenScore over time, after removing sticky disclosure text from the prior year, and orthogonalized to controls. GenScore is generated by GPTZero and reflects the estimated probability of GAI usage within the report. Observations to the right of the dotted line represent filings disseminated after ChatGPT was launched (i.e., post-GAI).

<div style="text-align: center;"><img src="https://pplines-online.bj.bcebos.com/deploy/official/paddleocr/pp-ocr-vl-15//8ebd7122-695f-4f25-8ad1-66e42b4fb01a/markdown_0/imgs/img_in_chart_box_145_313_1086_828.jpg?authorization=bce-auth-v1%2FALTAKzReLNvew3ySINYJ0fuAMN%2F2026-04-04T14%3A58%3A19Z%2F-1%2F%2F97329e979297ad06442fc3878f17d6193528a275a0245360403d94ec8f95a95e" alt="Image" width="76%" /></div>


#### Figure 2. Validation: Scores for Binary Classification

This chart plots precision, recall, and F1-scores for the binary classification tests discussed in Section 4.2. Each panel tabulates results using two binary classification methods. The first uses a threshold,  $ \gamma $, to designate reports as human-written versus GAI-modified. The second uses terciles to designate reports as high versus low GAI usage.  $ \mu $ is the fraction of sentences within a report that is GAI-modified.

#### Figure 4. GAI Usage by Topic

These figures portray average post-period changes in GenScore by topic. GenScore is the estimated probability of GAI usage at the sentence level, and  $ \Delta GenScore $ is the post-period GenScore minus the average GenScore for sentences assigned to the same topic in the pre-GAI period. Panel A portrays  $ \Delta GenScore $ in MD&A filings by LDA-determined topic. Panel B portrays  $ \Delta GenScore $ in MD&A filings by topic, just within the information technology industry.

#### Table 4. GAI Usage Tests

This table contains results of regressions that estimate GAI usage in disclosures. Panel A presents results of model (1), which regresses GenScore on an indicator for the post-GAI period starting 1 December 2022, for each of our four disclosure types. Panel B presents results of model (2), which replaces Post with annual indicators. Panel C presents results of model (3), which adds a time trend control. Panel D repeats Panel C, excluding sticky text from the prior year. Controls are untabulated in some panels and include firm size, book-to-market, analyst following, institutional ownership, leverage, segments, M&A, and loss. See Appendix A for variable definitions. T-statistics in brackets are based on standard errors clustered by year-quarter. *** indicates significance at 1%; ** at 5%; and * at 10%.

#### Table 5. GAI Usage Cross-Sectional Tests

This table contains results of regression model (4) of cross-sectional variation in GAI usage for reports excluding sticky text from the prior year, similar to the analyses in Table 4D. Panel A presents results with small firms as the cross-sectional variable. Panel B presents results for firms without an IR officer. Panel C presents results for firms with large absolute abnormal return during the period. Panel D presents results for firms with low size-adjusted audit fees. Controls are untabulated and include firm size, book-to-market, analyst following, institutional ownership, leverage, segments, M&A, and loss. See Appendix A for variable definitions. T-statistics in brackets are based on standard errors clustered by year-quarter. *** indicates significance at 1%; ** at 5%; and * at 10%.

#### Table 6. GAI Usage in IPO S1 Filings

This table contains results of regressions that estimate GAI usage in IPO firms’ S1 disclosures. It presents the results of model (3) but without fixed effects or controls, which regresses the disclosure section’s GenScore on an indicator for the post-GAI period starting 1 December 2022, for each of four disclosure sections in S1 filings: Business Description (BUS), MD&A, RF (Risk Factors), and PS (Prospectus Summary). See Appendix A for variable definitions. T-statistics in brackets are based on standard errors clustered by year-quarter. *** indicates significance at 1%; ** at 5%; and * at 10%.

#### Table 7. Additional Descriptive Analysis of GAI Usage

This table contains additional descriptive analysis of firms’ GAI usage. Panel A presents the distribution of  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $, which is the within-firm difference in GenScore between the post-GAI and pre-GAI periods. Panel B presents the number of firms first adopting in each year, where adoption is defined as falling into the top tercile of  $ \Delta GenScore $ (see details in Section 5.3). Panels C and D present descriptive statistics of adopters and non-adopters, using firm characteristics measured over 2019 through 2021. In Panel C, *** indicates significance at 1%; ** at 5%; and * at 10%. In Panel D, # indicates that the difference between the adopter and non-adopter groups is significant at 10% or better. Panel E presents the rank-order of  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $ by GICS2 industry. See Appendix A for variable definitions.

#### Table 8. GAI and Linguistic Properties in Researcher-Modified Reports

This table presents results of comparing the linguistic properties of our validation sample of human-generated financial reports versus our GAI-modified versions of the same reports. Panel A provides the average linguistic properties of the original human-written reports in the validation sample. Panel B summarizes the  $ \beta_1 $ coefficient estimates of model (5) with length, Fog, tone, and specificity as the dependent variable. We present results using the two replacement methods discussed in Section 4. Document-level replacement asks an LLM to revise an entire report at once, while sentence-level asks an LLM to revise one sentence at a time. In the sentence-level replacement, we replace 100% of the sentences (i.e.,  $ \mu = 100\% $). *** indicates significance at 1%; ** at 5%; and * at 10%.

### S1. Validation Tests LLM Details and Prompts

Just a few major LLMs dominate the market during our sample period; variants of OpenAI’s GPT (3.5, 4, 4o, and Microsoft Copilot which is based on GPT) were the only major players through early 2023 and continue to have an estimated 74% market share through August 2025. Google’s Gemini was introduced in March 2023 and is reported to have roughly 13.5% of the market. $ ^{31} $ Thus, our validation tests are based on GPT 3.5, GPT 4o, and Gemini.

We followed recommendations in de Kok (2025) for crafting high quality LLM prompts. The LLM parameters and prompts used in Section 4.1 are as follows:

• GPT 3.5 API Parameters: 3.5 Turbo; temperature = 0.7

Our prompt engineering tests indicate that GPT 3.5 is reticent to produce documents over 500 words. For the document-level replacements, we submit each report in ~500-word sections, ensuring that sentences remain complete.

• GPT 4o API Parameters: 4o mini; temperature = 0.7

o Same as above

• Gemini API Parameters: gemini-1.5-flash-001; temperature = 1.0

☐ Same as above

Prompt: Document-level replacement, RF

Your task is to write risk factor items for a 10-K for a firm. Here is a portion of their risk factor section from their 10-K:

Important Instructions: Carefully rewrite the risk factors provided above to improve the clarity of writing while retaining the main points. Use a similar format and length as the original risk factors. Make your rewritten risk factors longer than 50% of the length of the original risk factors and shorter than 110% of the length of the original risk factors. Like the original, begin each factor with a complete sentence in the first-person plural to summarize the risk, and then discuss the factor in a paragraph after that. Don't just copy what the company wrote. Instead, write these as you would if you were following the SEC's instructions for the risk factor statement: "Provide a discussion of the material factors

that make an investment in the registrant speculative or risky. Concisely explain in plain English how each risk affects the registrant." Do not provide introductory or summary comments at the beginning or end of the document; just rewrite the risk factors.

##### Prompt: Document-level replacement, MD&A

Your task is to write several paragraphs for an MD&A in a 10-K for a firm.

Here is a portion of their MD&A from their 10-K: ``7 Text''

Important Instructions: Carefully rewrite the MD&A paragraphs provided above to improve the clarity of writing while retaining the main points. Use a similar format and length as the original paragraphs. Make your rewritten paragraphs longer than 50% of the length of the original and shorter than 110% of the length of the original. Don't just copy what the company wrote. Instead, write these as you would if you were following the SEC's instructions for the MD&A: "Provide a narrative explanation to help investors understand a company's financial condition and operating results and how they relate to future performance. Do not use generic or boilerplate language."

##### Prompt: Document-level replacement, earnings press release

Your task is to write several paragraphs for a press release about a firm's earnings announcement.

Here is a portion of their press release: "PR Text"

Important Instructions: Carefully rewrite the press release provided above to improve the clarity of writing while retaining the main points. Use a similar format and length as the original paragraphs. Make your rewritten press release longer than 50% of the length of the original and shorter than 110% of the length of the original. Don't just copy what the company wrote. Instead, write it as you would if you were writing a press release to help investors and the financial media understand a company's quarterly financial condition and operating results.

##### Prompt: Document-level replacement, conference call

Your task is to write several paragraphs for an executive to read aloud during a conference call presentation about a firm's earnings announcement.

Here is a portion of their conference call presentation:  $ \cdots $CC Text''

Important Instructions: Carefully rewrite the conference call presentation paragraphs provided above to improve the clarity of writing while retaining the main points. Use a similar format and length as the original paragraphs. Make your rewritten paragraphs longer than 50% of the length of the original and shorter than 110% of the length of the original. Don't just copy what the company wrote. Instead, write these as you would if you were writing a script for an executive to read aloud during a conference call presentation to help investors and the financial media understand a company's quarterly financial condition and operating results.

## S2. Validation: Type I Error Rates and Type II Error Rates

## Type I Error Rates

As discussed in Section 4 of the paper, GenScore and model (1) could have abnormal statistical properties that produce type I errors. We investigate this threat by using randomization tests to estimate type I error rates in our validation data. Specifically, we randomly select one

year as the False-Post year for each firm and disclosure type, and estimate model (1) replacing Post with False-Post. We then repeat the steps 5,000 times to estimate a distribution of  $ \beta_1 $ and calculate the number of trials that find  $ \beta_1 \neq 0 $ at a 90% level of confidence, which we know to be type I errors. Untabulated results show that randomization tests produce average False-Post coefficients that are very close to zero and reject the null roughly 10% of the time. Thus, model (1) does not appear to have abnormal properties that will generate excessive type I errors.

Type II Error Rates: Power Analysis for GPT-4o and Gemini

This section repeats the power analysis from Section 4.1 and Table 3 of the paper but using GPT-4o (Table S2-1) and Gemini (Table S2-2) to generate GAI-modified reports. All inferences are very similar to those for GPT-3.5 as reported in the main paper.

#### S3. Validation: Performance of Binary Classifications

This section provides further details on our validation of a binary classification of GenScore, as discussed in Section 4.2 of the paper. Specifically, we convert GenScore to a binary measure and use F1 scores to characterize GPTZero’s ability to differentiate our 6,000 human-written reports from their 6,000 GAI-modified counterparts.

F1 scores can be illustrated using  $ 2 \times 2 $ matrices of true values (in our case, human-written and GAI-modified) and predicted values, as shown in Table S3-1A. A true positive prediction is when the document is GAI-modified (i.e., is “true”) and GPTZero identifies it as GAI-modified (i.e., is predicted to be true). Precision is the number of true positive predictions divided by the total number of positive predictions. Recall is the number of true positive predictions divided by the total number of GAI-modified reports. F1 is the harmonic mean of the two values, with values above 80% generally considered to be good and above 50% acceptable. However,

depending on a study's context, a researcher may prefer to focus on just one of precision or recall, or may prefer a higher or lower threshold of F1 acceptability.

As discussed in Section 4.2, we use two methods to convert GenScore into a binary classification: one based on a threshold,  $ \gamma $; and a second based on tercile splits. The first method uses the distribution of GenScore in human-written documents to select  $ \gamma $, above which a document is deemed to be GAI-modified. Specifically we first mitigate measurement error by de-meaning GenScore by firm and disclosure type in human-written reports, which mimics the functionality of the firm fixed effects in model (1), and is akin to Latona et al. (2024). We then set  $ \gamma $ as the  $ 90^{th} $ percentile of demeaned GenScore in human-written documents within each disclosure type, which equates to a 10% type I error rate in human-written-documents. All observations, human and GAI-modified, with demeaned GenScore >  $ \gamma $ are designated as GAI-modified. Our second method pools the human and GAI-modified reports and ranks GenScore into terciles, and then identifies the upper third as HighGAI, lower third as LowGAI, and omits the middle group.

Table S3-1B tabulates precision, recall, and F1-Scores for our first method based on  $ \gamma $.

The upper, middle, and lower rows report results based on GPT-3.5, GPT-4o, and Gemini. The left columns tabulate results for complete reports and right columns tabulate results after excluding sticky text. Precision is above 0.5 in all specifications, indicating that more than half of predicted GAI-modified documents are GAI-modified. Recall is good for high levels of  $ \mu $ but suffers as  $ \mu $ declines. In the full reports, F1-scores are acceptable for all three LLMs when  $ \mu $ is 10% or greater. Once sticky text is removed, F1-scores are acceptable when  $ \mu $ is 5% or greater.

Table S3-1C tabulates precision, recall, and F1-Scores for our second method based on tercile splits. Precision improves marginally across all specifications while recall improves

substantially, and the F1-scores are now acceptable for all levels of  $ \mu $. Stronger performance when using tercile splits is expected, given that designating documents as “high” versus “low” GAI usage is less demanding than a designation of zero versus some GAI usage.

### S4. Results of cross-sectional tests using the complete report text

This section repeats the GAI Usage Cross-Sectional Tests tabulated in Table 5, but for the complete reports rather than excluding sticky text. The design is as discussed in Section 5.1, and results are briefly discussed there as well.

### S5. Implementation of Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) to MD&A

We apply Latent Dirichlet Allocation (LDA) to the MD&A sections of 10K and 10Q filings. To prepare the text for topic modeling, we implement the following preprocessing pipeline:

1) Separate text into sentences, convert to lowercase, and remove punctuation, numbers, and other non-alphabetic characters.

2) Separate sentences into words, remove stopwords (using NLTK's English stopword list), exclude words shorter than three characters, and lemmatize words using WordNet.

3) Exclude extremely rare words (those appearing in fewer than 100 documents) and extremely common terms (appearing in more than 95% of documents), ensuring topics are not dominated by noise or boilerplate language. We also remove common time-related words (e.g., January, February, month, quarterly, annual, one, two, three, etc.).

4) Convert the cleaned corpus into a bag-of-words representation.

We implement LDA using gensim’s LdaModel. Candidate models are estimated with 10 to 70 topics, in increments of 5. After evaluating each model based on coherence scores (C_v) and interpretability of top-weighted words, we selected a 55-topic model for our main analysis and a 30-topic model for our industry-specific analysis. We labeled each topic by interpreting the top words and validating with representative sentences. We refer to Dyer et al.’s (2017) 150-topic framework to guide labeling, relying on overlap in keywords where available, the order of top-weighted keywords, and anchor words. When a topic does not have a clear counterpart in Dyer et al.’s framework, we assign labels based on our interpretation of top words and representative sentences and using input from ChatGPT, while maintaining consistency with related Dyer et al. categories where possible. In the full-sample analysis, 18 of the topics did not have a clear counterpart from Dyer et al. Two of the LDA topics matched seemed to be variations of the same topic, which we labeled Performance Growth. We distinguish them based on tone, as it is significantly different between them, labeling one “More Positive” and the other “More Negative.”

After training the LDA model at the document level, we infer topic loadings for each sentence in the MD&A based on the words within that sentence, adapting Dyer et al.'s (2017) paragraph-level inference to the sentence level. Each sentence is then assigned to the topic with the highest loading. This procedure yields groups of sentences clustered by their dominant topic, which we use to calculate topic-level changes in GenScore.

## S6. Changes in Financial Reporting Properties of Validation Sample

This section repeats the analysis of changes in financial reporting properties of the validation sample reports from Section 6 and Table 8 of the paper but using GPT-4o (Table S6-

1A) and Gemini (Table S6-1B) to generate GAI-modified reports. Inferences are similar to those for GPT-3.5 as reported in the main paper, except for the following items: GPT-4o increases the length of MD&A, CC, and PR reports under the Document Replacement method, Gemini increases the length of RF reports under the Document Replacement method, and Gemini decreases the Fog score of RF, MD&A, and PR reports under the Document Replacement method.

#### S7. Tabulation of Robustness Tests of GAI and Linguistic Features

Section 6.2 mentions robustness tests in which we repeat Table 9 after limiting the sample to only firms that likely use GAI in the post period. This section further describes those analyses, and the results are presented in Table S7-1.

We identify firms that are likely GAI users for each disclosure type using a within-sample ranking approach similar to the one developed in Section 4.2 of the paper. To eliminate fixed firm-specific measurement error in GenScore, we subtract each firm’s pre-GAI average GenScore from each post-GAI report, by disclosure type. We then rank the resulting values,  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $, into terciles in the post-GAI period. The largest tercile of  $ \Delta GenScore_{it} $ are designated as high GAI usage ( $ HighGAI_{it} = 1 $). Finally, we limit the sample to observations from firms that are designated HighGAI at least once for a given disclosure type. We repeat model (6) using these truncated samples that range from 48% to 60% of the original sample size.

Table S7-1 summarizes the results, and inferences are very similar to Table 9.

### Table S3-1. Validation: Binary GenScore Classification

This table corresponds to the discussion in Section S3 of the Online Appendix. Panel A is an example confusion matrix and shows the formulas for precision, recall, and F1-scores. Panel B tabulates precision, recall, and F1-scores when classifying documents as human-written versus GAI-modified using a threshold approach. Panel C tabulates the same but when classifying documents as low versus high AI usage using tercile splits.

<div style="text-align: center;"><div style="text-align: center;">Panel A: Illustration of confusion matrix</div> </div>




<table border=1 style='margin: auto; word-wrap: break-word;'><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'></td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>True Value: GAI</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>True Value: Human</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Predicted Value: GAI</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>True Positive (TP)</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>False Positive (FP)</td></tr><tr><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>Predicted Value: Human</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>False Negative (FN)</td><td style='text-align: center; word-wrap: break-word;'>True Negative (TN)</td></tr></table>

• Precision = TP / (TP + FP)

• Recall = TP / (TP + FN)

• F1-score = harmonic mean = (2 x Precision x Recall) / (Precision + Recall)

This table corresponds to the discussion in Section S4 of the Online Appendix and presents results of estimating model (4) from Section 5.1 of cross-sectional variation in GAI usage, using the sample with full report text (not excluding sticky text). Panels A, B, C, and D present results with the cross-sectional variable of small firms, firms without an IR officer, firms with large absolute abnormal return during the period, and firms with low size-adjusted audit fees, respectively. Controls are untabulated and include firm size, book-to-market, analyst following, institutional ownership, leverage, segments, M&A, and loss. See Appendix A for variable definitions. T-statistics in brackets are based on standard errors clustered by year-quarter. *** indicates significance at 1%; ** at 5%; and * at 10%.
